---
title: "The reverse-time SDE and score matching"
description: "How noise is turned back into data: the score ∇ log p(x), Langevin dynamics, Anderson's reverse-time SDE dx = [f − g² ∇ log p_t] dt + g dW̄ (what running Brownian motion backwards does and does not mean), the probability-flow ODE, score matching and denoising score matching, why predicting the noise is estimating the score, and a numpy demonstration that samples a 2-D Gaussian mixture from pure noise with the exact score."
tags: [topic, diffusion]
aliases: ["score function", "score matching", "denoising score matching", "reverse SDE", "reverse-time diffusion", "reverse Brownian motion", "probability flow ODE", "Langevin dynamics", "annealed Langevin dynamics", "NCSN"]
---

*Beyond the lectures · [[8-diffusion-models/index|Diffusion models]] · sourced from Anderson (1982), Hyvärinen (2005), Vincent (2011), Song & Ermon (2019), Ho et al. (2020) and Song et al. (2021) · builds on [[8-diffusion-models/brownian-motion-and-forward-diffusion|the forward process]] and [[2-calculus-and-optimization/gradients-and-partial-derivatives|gradients]]*

> [!abstract] In one breath
> The forward process turns data into noise by Brownian motion. A theorem of Anderson (1982) says that the same process, viewed with time running backwards, is **again** a diffusion: $\mathrm dx=[f(x,t)-g(t)^2\,\nabla_x\log p_t(x)]\,\mathrm dt+g(t)\,\mathrm d\bar W$. It still injects fresh Brownian noise; what makes it flow from noise to data is the extra drift $-g^2\nabla_x\log p_t$, which pushes toward higher probability. The only unknown is the **score** $\nabla_x\log p_t(x)$ of the noised data, and it can be learned by **denoising**: the best predictor of the noise $\varepsilon$ in $x_t$ is (up to a factor $-1/\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}$) exactly that score. Train a network to predict noise, plug it into the reverse SDE — or into the noise-free **probability-flow ODE** with the same marginals — and integrate from $\mathcal N(0,\mathbf I)$ back to $t=0$. That is a diffusion model.

## What it is

Generating data means sampling from an unknown $p_{\text{data}}$. The forward process of the [[8-diffusion-models/brownian-motion-and-forward-diffusion|previous page]] connects $p_{\text{data}}=p_0$ to a simple $p_T\approx\mathcal N(0,\mathbf I)$ through a family of noised distributions $p_t$. If the process can be **reversed**, sampling is easy: draw $x_T\sim\mathcal N(0,\mathbf I)$ and run the reversal. Song et al. (2021) summarize it: "Creating noise from data is easy; creating data from noise is generative modeling."

> [!definition] Definition — score
> The **score** of a density $p$ is the gradient of its log, $s(x)=\nabla_x\log p(x)$: a vector field pointing in the direction in which the density increases fastest. For noised data, $s(x,t)=\nabla_x\log p_t(x)$ for each noise level $t$.

## What you should know

- The score of a Gaussian, $\nabla_x\log\mathcal N(x;\mu,\sigma^2\mathbf I)=-(x-\mu)/\sigma^2$, and that the score never needs the normalizing constant of $p$.
- Langevin dynamics $x\leftarrow x+\tfrac\epsilon2\nabla_x\log p(x)+\sqrt\epsilon\,z$ samples $p$ using only the score, and why it fails between well-separated modes.
- **Anderson's reverse-time SDE** $\mathrm dx=[f-g^2\nabla_x\log p_t]\,\mathrm dt+g\,\mathrm d\bar W$: time runs from $T$ to 0, $\mathrm dt<0$, $\bar W$ is a Brownian motion in reversed time. What is reversed (the distribution of whole paths) and what is not (the noise itself).
- The **probability-flow ODE** $\mathrm dx=[f-\tfrac12g^2\nabla_x\log p_t]\,\mathrm dt$: deterministic, same marginals $p_t$ as the SDE.
- **Denoising score matching**: regressing $s_\theta(\tilde x)$ onto $\nabla_{\tilde x}\log q_\sigma(\tilde x\mid x)=-(\tilde x-x)/\sigma^2$ learns the score of the noised data, without second derivatives.
- **Noise prediction = score estimation**: for $x_t=\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}x_0+\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\varepsilon$, $\nabla_{x_t}\log q(x_t\mid x_0)=-\varepsilon/\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}$, so $s_\theta=-\varepsilon_\theta/\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}$ and DDPM's loss is a weighted denoising score-matching loss.
- Why many noise levels: scores are ill-defined or badly estimated where there is no data; noise fills space and connects the modes (Song & Ermon, 2019).

## The score

For a Gaussian $\mathcal N(\mu,\sigma^2\mathbf I)$, $\log p(x)=-\|x-\mu\|^2/(2\sigma^2)+\text{const}$, so $\nabla_x\log p(x)=-(x-\mu)/\sigma^2$: an arrow back to the mean, longer the farther away you are and the narrower the Gaussian. For a mixture $p(x)=\sum_k w_k\,\mathcal N(x;\mu_k,\sigma^2\mathbf I)$,

$$
\nabla_x\log p(x)=\sum_k r_k(x)\left(-\frac{x-\mu_k}{\sigma^2}\right),\qquad r_k(x)=\frac{w_k\,\mathcal N(x;\mu_k,\sigma^2\mathbf I)}{\sum_j w_j\,\mathcal N(x;\mu_j,\sigma^2\mathbf I)},
$$

a responsibility-weighted average of the components' arrows.

The decisive property: if $p(x)=\tilde p(x)/Z$ with an unknown constant $Z$, then $\nabla_x\log p=\nabla_x\log\tilde p$ — the constant disappears. Neural densities are hard to normalize; scores are not. A network $s_\theta(x)$ can output any vector field.

x="232.4" y="20.8" width="187.6" height="187.6"/></clipPath></defs></svg> <figcaption><b>The score is a vector field pointing uphill in log-density.</b> Arrows: the exact score ∇ log p_t(x) of a two-component Gaussian mixture (lengths capped for display), over its density (grey contours). Left, the data distribution: arrows point into the nearest blob, and between the blobs they split. Right, after VP noising to t = 0.25: the density is broader and the field is smooth everywhere — which is why scores of noised data are easier to learn.</figcaption></figure>

## Sampling with the score: Langevin dynamics

Given only $\nabla_x\log p$, **Langevin dynamics** (Song & Ermon, 2019, eq. 4) produces samples of $p$:

$$
x_k=x_{k-1}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\,\nabla_x\log p(x_{k-1})+\sqrt{\epsilon}\;z_k,\qquad z_k\sim\mathcal N(0,\mathbf I).
$$

It is [[2-calculus-and-optimization/gradient-descent|gradient ascent]] on $\log p$ plus noise; the noise keeps it from collapsing onto the mode, and as $\epsilon\to0$ and the number of steps $\to\infty$ the iterates are exact samples. (Song et al., 2021, write the same rule as $x+\epsilon\,s+\sqrt{2\epsilon}\,z$.)

Song & Ermon point out two failures when the score is used on the **clean** data distribution. Real data lie near low-dimensional manifolds where $\nabla_x\log p$ is not even defined, and in empty regions there are no samples to learn the score from. And for a mixture of well-separated modes, the score inside each mode does not depend on the mixture weights, so Langevin dynamics cannot get the weights right in reasonable time. Their fix — **noise**. Perturb the data with Gaussian noise at a sequence of scales $\sigma_1>\dots>\sigma_L$, learn the score at every scale with one **noise-conditional score network** $s_\theta(x,\sigma)$, and run **annealed Langevin dynamics**: $T$ Langevin steps at each scale with step size $\alpha_i=\epsilon\,\sigma_i^2/\sigma_L^2$, from the largest noise down (their Algorithm 1). At large noise the modes overlap and samples can move between them; as the noise is reduced they settle into the right modes in the right proportions.

height="101.9"/></clipPath><clipPath id="fe3f984"><rect x="374.0" y="29.6" width="101.9" height="101.9"/></clipPath></defs></svg> <figcaption><b>Why noise levels matter</b> (the example of Song and Ermon, 2019: weights 0.2 and 0.8, 2,000 samples each, 500 drawn, all scores exact). Plain Langevin dynamics on the data score, started uniformly in a box, finds both modes but not their weights: the score inside each blob does not depend on the weights, and nothing moves samples across the empty gap. Annealed Langevin (10 noise levels from 10 to 0.1) and the reverse-time VP SDE both start where the noised distributions overlap and recover the 0.2 / 0.8 split.</figcaption></figure>

```python
x = rng.uniform(-8, 8, (n, 2))
step = 0.1
for _ in range(1000):                  # plain Langevin, exact data score
    x = x + 0.5 * step * mixture_score(x, 1e-9, w, mu, s2) + np.sqrt(step) * rng.standard_normal(x.shape)
```

In the run above (Song & Ermon's mixture $\tfrac15\mathcal N((-5,-5),\mathbf I)+\tfrac45\mathcal N((5,5),\mathbf I)$, 2,000 samples, exact scores), the share of samples in the lower-left mode is 0.20 for exact sampling, **0.49** for plain Langevin (its starting split, essentially unchanged), 0.21 for annealed Langevin with 10 levels from $\sigma=10$ to $0.1$, and 0.20 for the reverse-time SDE below.

## Running the diffusion backwards

Annealed Langevin uses a ladder of noise levels. The continuous version replaces the ladder by the forward SDE $\mathrm dx=f(x,t)\,\mathrm dt+g(t)\,\mathrm dW$ and asks: what process goes from $p_T$ back to $p_0$?

> [!key] Theorem — reverse-time diffusion (Anderson, 1982; Song et al., 2021, eq. 6)
> The forward diffusion, run backwards in time from $T$ to 0, is again a diffusion, given by the **reverse-time SDE**
> $$
> \mathrm dx=\big[f(x,t)-g(t)^2\,\nabla_x\log p_t(x)\big]\,\mathrm dt+g(t)\,\mathrm d\bar W,
> $$
> where $\bar W$ is a standard Brownian motion when time flows backwards and $\mathrm dt$ is an infinitesimal **negative** time step. Started from $x(T)\sim p_T$, its solution has distribution $p_t$ at every $t$, and $x(0)\sim p_0$.

"Reverse Brownian motion" is a good informal name for this, as long as it is clear what is reversed:

| | Forward SDE | Reverse-time SDE |
| --- | --- | --- |
| time | $0\to T$ | $T\to0$ ($\mathrm dt<0$) |
| drift | $f(x,t)$ | $f(x,t)-g(t)^2\nabla_x\log p_t(x)$ |
| noise | $g(t)\,\mathrm dW$ | $g(t)\,\mathrm d\bar W$ — **new** noise, same strength |
| distribution at time $t$ | $p_t$ | $p_t$ — the same |

- **Reversed:** the arrow of time and the statistics of whole trajectories. A movie of the reverse process is indistinguishable, in distribution, from a movie of the forward process played backwards.
- **Not reversed:** the noise. Individual random kicks are not undone — they cannot be, since they are not recorded. The reverse process draws **fresh** Brownian increments, as many and as strong as the forward one.
- **What does the work:** the extra drift $-g^2\nabla_x\log p_t$. Run forward, Brownian noise spreads probability out; to undo the spreading while still adding noise, the process needs a pull toward high density that beats it. Since $\mathrm dt<0$, the term $-g^2\nabla\log p_t\,\mathrm dt$ is a step **along** the score.

> [!derivation]- Why the score appears (marginals only)
> The density of the forward SDE obeys the Fokker–Planck equation
> $$
> \partial_t p_t=-\nabla\!\cdot(f\,p_t)+\tfrac12g^2\,\Delta p_t .
> $$
> Use $\Delta p=\nabla\!\cdot(\nabla p)=\nabla\!\cdot(p\,\nabla\log p)$. Writing $\tfrac12g^2\Delta p_t=g^2\Delta p_t-\tfrac12g^2\Delta p_t$,
> $$
> \partial_t p_t=-\nabla\!\cdot\big[(f-g^2\nabla\log p_t)\,p_t\big]-\tfrac12g^2\,\Delta p_t .
> $$
> Substitute reversed time $\tau=T-t$, $q_\tau=p_{T-\tau}$: then $\partial_\tau q_\tau=\nabla\!\cdot\big[(f-g^2\nabla\log p_t)\,q_\tau\big]+\tfrac12g^2\Delta q_\tau$, which is the Fokker–Planck equation of an SDE in $\tau$ with drift $-(f-g^2\nabla\log p_t)$ and diffusion $g$ — the reverse-time SDE written in forward-running $\tau$. Writing all of $\tfrac12g^2\Delta p_t$ as a divergence instead gives the probability-flow ODE below. This shows the **marginals** match; Anderson's theorem is stronger (the whole path distribution matches).

## The probability-flow ODE

The same rewriting with no noise left over gives a deterministic process with the **same marginal densities** $p_t$ (Song et al., 2021, eq. 13):

$$
\mathrm dx=\Big[f(x,t)-\tfrac12\,g(t)^2\,\nabla_x\log p_t(x)\Big]\,\mathrm dt .
$$

Half the score push and no noise, instead of the full push plus noise. Each starting point $x_T$ is mapped to one $x_0$, so the ODE is an invertible map between noise and data; with a learned score it is a neural ODE. It can be integrated with large adaptive steps — Song et al. report cutting function evaluations by over 90% with a black-box ODE solver without visible quality loss — and it allows exact likelihoods. The SDE and ODE produce **different paths** but the same distribution at every $t$. Faster samplers built on this ODE are on the [[8-diffusion-models/fast-sampling-ddim-and-ode-solvers|fast-sampling page]].

## Learning the score

The reverse SDE needs $\nabla_x\log p_t$ for all $t$, and $p_t$ is unknown. Score matching estimates a score from samples alone.

**Score matching** (Hyvärinen, 2005) minimizes $\tfrac12\mathbb E_p\|s_\theta(x)-\nabla_x\log p(x)\|^2$. The unknown true score can be integrated away, leaving
$$
\mathbb E_{p}\Big[\operatorname{tr}\big(\nabla_x s_\theta(x)\big)+\tfrac12\|s_\theta(x)\|^2\Big]+\text{const},
$$
but the trace of the Jacobian costs one backward pass per input dimension — hopeless for images.

**Denoising score matching** (Vincent, 2011) avoids it. Corrupt each sample, $\tilde x=x+\sigma\varepsilon$, and regress onto the score of the **corruption kernel**, which is known:
$$
\tfrac12\,\mathbb E_{x\sim p,\ \tilde x\sim q_\sigma(\tilde x\mid x)}\big\|s_\theta(\tilde x)-\nabla_{\tilde x}\log q_\sigma(\tilde x\mid x)\big\|^2,\qquad \nabla_{\tilde x}\log q_\sigma(\tilde x\mid x)=-\frac{\tilde x-x}{\sigma^2}=-\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma}.
$$
The minimizer is the score of the **noised** distribution, $s_{\theta^*}(\tilde x)=\nabla_{\tilde x}\log q_\sigma(\tilde x)$. In words: a network trained to point from the noisy sample back toward the clean one, averaged over everything the clean one could have been, learns the score of the noisy data. Song & Ermon train $s_\theta(x,\sigma)$ this way at all scales at once, weighting scale $\sigma$ by $\sigma^2$; Song et al. (2021, eq. 7) make the scale continuous, $\mathbb E_t\,\lambda(t)\,\mathbb E\|s_\theta(x(t),t)-\nabla_{x(t)}\log p_{0t}(x(t)\mid x(0))\|^2$.

> [!key] Predicting the noise is estimating the score
> For the VP / DDPM forward process, $x_t=\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}\,x_0+\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\,\varepsilon$, so
> $$
> \nabla_{x_t}\log q(x_t\mid x_0)=-\frac{x_t-\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}\,x_0}{1-\bar\alpha_t}=-\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}} .
> $$
> Weighting the denoising score-matching loss by $1-\bar\alpha_t$ (Song et al., 2021, eq. 3) gives $\|\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\,s_\theta+\varepsilon\|^2=\|\varepsilon-\varepsilon_\theta\|^2$ with
> $$
> \varepsilon_\theta(x_t,t)=-\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\;s_\theta(x_t,t),
> $$
> which is exactly DDPM's simple loss $L_{\text{simple}}$ (Ho et al., 2020, eq. 14). A network that predicts the added noise is a scaled score model, and DDPM's sampler is one particular discretization of the reverse-time VP SDE (Song et al., 2021, Sec. 4.1). See the [[8-diffusion-models/ddpm|DDPM page]].

## A complete demonstration with the exact score

For one family of data distributions the score of every $p_t$ is known in closed form: **Gaussian mixtures**. If a component is $\mathcal N(\mu_k,s^2\mathbf I)$, the VP forward process maps it to $x_t=m(t)\,x_0+\sqrt{1-m(t)^2}\,\varepsilon$ with $m(t)=e^{-\frac12\int_0^t\beta}$, i.e. to $\mathcal N\big(m\mu_k,\ (m^2s^2+1-m^2)\mathbf I\big)$. So $p_t$ is again a mixture with the same weights, and its score is the mixture formula above. Replacing the trained network by this exact score isolates the sampler: if the reverse SDE is right, it must turn $\mathcal N(0,\mathbf I)$ into the mixture.

The code below is the code that produced the figures on this page (`tools/figs_diff_a.py`):

```python
B_MIN, B_MAX = 0.1, 20.0                     # Song et al. (2021)

def beta(t):
    return B_MIN + t * (B_MAX - B_MIN)

def vp_mean_coef(t):                         # m(t) = exp(-1/2 ∫β)
    return np.exp(-0.5 * (B_MIN * t + 0.5 * (B_MAX - B_MIN) * t**2))

def mixture_score(x, t, w, mu, s2):
    """Exact score of a Gaussian-mixture p_t."""
    m = vp_mean_coef(t)
    var = m**2 * s2 + 1 - m**2               # per-component variance
    diff = x[:, None, :] - m * mu[None]      # (n, K, d)
    logp = (np.log(w)[None] - 0.5 * (diff**2).sum(-1) / var
            - 0.5 * mu.shape[1] * np.log(var))
    r = np.exp(logp - logp.max(1, keepdims=True))
    r /= r.sum(1, keepdims=True)             # responsibilities
    return -(r[..., None] * diff).sum(1) / var

def reverse_sde(x, w, mu, s2, n_steps=1000, t_eps=1e-3, rng=None, keep=()):
    """Euler-Maruyama on the reverse-time VP SDE."""
    ts = np.linspace(1.0, t_eps, n_steps + 1)
    snaps = {}
    for i in range(n_steps):
        t, dt = ts[i], ts[i] - ts[i + 1]     # dt > 0, time runs down
        f = -0.5 * beta(t) * x
        g2 = beta(t)
        drift = f - g2 * mixture_score(x, t, w, mu, s2)
        x = x - drift * dt + np.sqrt(g2 * dt) * rng.standard_normal(x.shape)
        if i + 1 in keep:
            snaps[i + 1] = x.copy()
    return x, snaps

def prob_flow_ode(x, w, mu, s2, n_steps=1000, t_eps=1e-3, keep=()):
    """Euler on the probability-flow ODE, backwards."""
    ts = np.linspace(1.0, t_eps, n_steps + 1)
    snaps = {}
    for i in range(n_steps):
        t, dt = ts[i], ts[i] - ts[i + 1]
        drift = -0.5 * beta(t) * x - 0.5 * beta(t) * mixture_score(x, t, w, mu, s2)
        x = x - drift * dt                   # no noise term
        if i + 1 in keep:
            snaps[i + 1] = x.copy()
    return x, snaps
```

Two details carry the theory. The loop variable `dt` is the positive step size, and the update is `x - drift * dt`: that is the reverse SDE's $\mathrm dt<0$. The noise term `np.sqrt(g2 * dt) * z` is a fresh Brownian increment of the same size as in the forward process, while the ODE has none and only half the score term. Both stop at $t=10^{-3}$ rather than 0, because the score of nearly noise-free data becomes very steep.

```python
rng = np.random.default_rng(2)
w, mu, s2 = ring_mixture()                   # 8 blobs, radius 2, std 0.12
xT = rng.standard_normal((2000, 2))
keep = (400, 700, 850, 950)
xs, snaps = reverse_sde(xT.copy(), w, mu, s2, rng=rng, keep=keep)
xo, _ = prob_flow_ode(xT.copy(), w, mu, s2)
```

Sans Pro', 'DejaVu Sans'; text-anchor: middle; fill: currentColor" x="457.8" y="12.9" transform="rotate(-0 457.8 12.9)">ODE, same start</text></g></g></g><defs><clipPath id="f66f2b2"><rect x="7.2" y="18.9" width="58.5" height="58.5"/></clipPath><clipPath id="f66f2b3"><rect x="77.4" y="18.9" width="58.5" height="58.5"/></clipPath><clipPath id="f66f2b4"><rect x="147.6" y="18.9" width="58.5" height="58.5"/></clipPath></defs></svg> <figcaption><b>Generating the ring from pure noise</b> with the exact score: 2,000 standard-normal points (500 drawn) pushed through 1,000 Euler–Maruyama steps of the reverse-time VP SDE (blue), and the same starting points through the probability-flow ODE (red, last panel). Structure appears late — most of it after t ≈ 0.3, when the noise is small. The SDE puts between 235 and 274 points in each of the eight blobs (expected 250).</figcaption></figure>

**Result.** All 2,000 SDE samples land on the ring, with 235 to 274 in each of the eight blobs (250 expected); the ODE gives 241 to 260 per blob. The mean distance from a sample to the nearest blob centre is 0.153 for the SDE and 0.149 for the ODE, against $0.12\sqrt{\pi/2}=0.150$ for exact samples of a 2-D Gaussian with standard deviation 0.12. With the exact score, 1,000 Euler steps reproduce the distribution — what remains hard in practice is **learning** the score of images, which is the job of the network on the [[8-diffusion-models/ddpm|DDPM page]].

> [!trap] Common mistakes
> - **Sign of time.** In the reverse SDE $\mathrm dt$ is negative. With a positive step size $h$, the update is $x\leftarrow x-[f-g^2 s]\,h+g\sqrt h\,z$; writing $+[f-g^2s]h$ runs the forward process again.
> - **Leaving out the noise.** Dropping $g\,\mathrm d\bar W$ from the reverse SDE but keeping the full $g^2$ score term gives neither the SDE nor the ODE, and over-sharpens samples. The noise-free version needs the factor $\tfrac12$.
> - **Expecting to retrace a path.** The reverse process does not undo a particular forward path; it samples a new one from the same distribution. The ODE is deterministic, but its paths are not the forward SDE's paths either.
> - **Score sign and scale.** The score target is $-\varepsilon/\sigma$ (or $-\varepsilon/\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}$), not $+\varepsilon$. Mixing up "predict the noise" and "predict the score" flips the drift.
> - **Integrating to exactly $t=0$.** Near $t=0$ the score of data concentrated on a thin set blows up; samplers stop at a small $t_\varepsilon$ (here $10^{-3}$) or use a final denoising step.
> - **Plain Langevin on multimodal data.** Scores at a single noise level cannot fix mode proportions; noise levels (annealing, or the SDE) are not optional.

> [!question]- Check yourself
> 1. What is $\nabla_x\log p(x)$ for $p=\mathcal N(\mu,\sigma^2\mathbf I)$, and what is it at $x=\mu$?
> 2. In the reverse-time SDE, why is the score term $g^2\nabla\log p_t$ but only $\tfrac12g^2\nabla\log p_t$ in the probability-flow ODE?
> 3. A DDPM network outputs $\varepsilon_\theta(x_t,t)$ at a step with $\bar\alpha_t=0.36$. What is its implied score estimate?
> 4. Why does Langevin dynamics with the exact data score put about half of the samples in each mode of $\tfrac15\mathcal N((-5,-5),\mathbf I)+\tfrac45\mathcal N((5,5),\mathbf I)$ when started uniformly?
>
> > [!solution]- Answers
> > 1. $-(x-\mu)/\sigma^2$; zero at the mean.
> > 2. The SDE adds fresh noise that spreads probability by $\tfrac12g^2\Delta p$; the drift has to undo the forward spreading **and** this new spreading, $\tfrac12g^2+\tfrac12g^2=g^2$. The ODE adds no noise and only has to undo the forward spreading.
> > 3. $s_\theta=-\varepsilon_\theta/\sqrt{1-0.36}=-\varepsilon_\theta/0.8=-1.25\,\varepsilon_\theta$.
> > 4. Inside each mode the score is (almost exactly) that mode's own $-(x-\mu_k)$, independent of the weights $\tfrac15,\tfrac45$. Each sample slides into the mode on its side of the box, and crossing the low-density gap between them essentially never happens, so the starting 50/50 split survives.
